**Dimension.** The linear form $\operatorname{tr}:\mathcal{M}_3(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is nonzero (e.g. $\operatorname{tr}(E_{11})=1$), so it is surjective. By rank-nullity, $\dim F=9-1=8$.

**Conclusion.** $F$ is a hyperplane of $\mathcal{M}_3(\mathbb{R})$, of dimension $8$.

@@@ vector-symmetric-skew.md
---
title: Symmetric and skew-symmetric matrices
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: symmetric, skew-symmetric, direct sum
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $S=\{M\in\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{R}) : M^T=M\}$ and $A=\{M\in\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{R}) : M^T=-M\}$. Show that $S$ and $A$ are subspaces and that $\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{R})=S\oplus A$.

## Hint

Show $S\cap A=\{0\}$ and every matrix decomposes as $M=\frac{M+M^T}{2}+\frac{M-M^T}{2}$.

## Solution

**Subspaces.** $S$ and $A$ are defined by linear conditions ($M^T-M=0$ and $M^T+M=0$), hence are kernels of linear maps, hence subspaces.

**Intersection.** If $M\in S\cap A$, then $M^T=M$ and $M^T=-M$, so $M=-M$, i.e. $M=0$.

**Decomposition.** For any $M$:
$$M = \underbrace{\frac{M+M^T}{2}}_{\in S} + \underbrace{\frac{M-M^T}{2}}_{\in A}.$$
Indeed, the first term is symmetric and the second is skew-symmetric.

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{R})=S\oplus A$. Their dimensions are $\frac{n(n+1)}{2}$ and $\frac{n(n-1)}{2}$, summing to $n^2$.

@@@ vector-polynomial-subspace.md
---
title: A subspace of polynomials with a root condition
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomial subspace, evaluation, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F=\{P\in\mathbb{R}_3[X] : P(1)=0\}$. Show that $F$ is a subspace of $\mathbb{R}_3[X]$ and compute its dimension.

## Hint

The evaluation map $P\mapsto P(1)$ is a linear form. It is surjective. Use rank-nullity.

## Solution

**Linearity.** The map $\varphi:P\mapsto P(1)$ is linear (evaluation is linear). So $F=\ker\varphi$ is a subspace.

**Surjectivity.** $\varphi$ is nonzero (e.g. $\varphi(1)=1$), hence surjective onto $\mathbb{R}$.

**Dimension.** $\dim F=\dim\mathbb{R}_3[X]-1=4-1=3$.

**Explicit basis.** $P\in F$ iff $P$ is divisible by $X-1$, i.e. $P=(X-1)Q$ with $Q\in\mathbb{R}_2[X]$. A basis is $(X-1, (X-1)X, (X-1)X^2)$.

**Conclusion.** $F$ is a hyperplane of $\mathbb{R}_3[X]$, of dimension $3$.

@@@ vector-linear-map-injective.md
---
title: Injective linear map and kernel
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: injectivity, kernel, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to F$ be a linear map. Show that $f$ is injective if and only if $\ker f=\{0\}$.

## Hint

For the forward direction, use $f(0)=0$. For the converse, assume $f(u)=f(v)$ and use linearity.

## Solution

**If $f$ is injective.** $f(0)=0$ because $f$ is linear. If $u\in\ker f$, then $f(u)=0=f(0)$, so $u=0$ by injectivity. Hence $\ker f=\{0\}$.

**Conversely.** Suppose $\ker f=\{0\}$. If $f(u)=f(v)$, then $f(u-v)=0$, so $u-v\in\ker f=\{0\}$, i.e. $u=v$. So $f$ is injective.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is injective iff $\ker f=\{0\}$.

@@@ vector-surjective-linear-map.md
---
title: Surjective linear map and image
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: surjectivity, image, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be defined by $f(x,y,z)=(x+y, y+z)$. Show that $f$ is surjective.

## Hint

Find preimages of $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$.

## Solution

**Preimages.** $f(1,0,0)=(1,0)$ and $f(0,0,1)=(0,1)$.

**Surjectivity.** For any $(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^2$, $f(a,0,b)=(a,b)$. So every target is hit.

**Kernel.** $x+y=0$ and $y+z=0$ give $y=-x$, $z=x$, so $\ker f=\operatorname{span}((1,-1,1))$.

**Rank-nullity check.** $\dim\ker f+\dim\operatorname{Im} f=1+2=3$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is surjective with kernel of dimension $1$.

@@@ vector-matrix-column-space.md
---
title: Column space and row space
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: column space, row space, rank
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 2 & 4 & 6 \\ 3 & 6 & 9 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Describe the column space and row space of $A$.

## Hint

All rows are proportional. The column space is spanned by the columns.

## Solution

**Row space.** All rows are multiples of $(1,2,3)$, so the row space is $\operatorname{span}((1,2,3))$, dimension $1$.

**Column space.** All columns are multiples of $(1,2,3)^T$, so the column space is $\operatorname{span}((1,2,3)^T)$, dimension $1$.

**Rank.** $\operatorname{rank}(A)=1$.

**Kernel.** $\dim\ker A=3-1=2$. Solve $x+2y+3z=0$: basis $(-2,1,0),(-3,0,1)$.

**Conclusion.** Row and column spaces are both lines, and $\ker A$ is a plane.

@@@ vector-dimension-quotient.md
---
title: Dimension of a quotient space
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: quotient space, dimension, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional vector space and $F$ a subspace. Show that $\dim(E/F)=\dim E-\dim F$.

## Hint

Choose a basis of $F$, extend it to a basis of $E$, and show the images of the added vectors form a basis of $E/F$.

## Solution

**Setup.** Let $(e_1,\ldots,e_k)$ be a basis of $F$ and extend to a basis $(e_1,\ldots,e_k,e_{k+1},\ldots,e_n)$ of $E$.

**Span of the quotient.** Every vector $v\in E$ decomposes as $v=\sum_{i=1}^k a_i e_i + \sum_{j=k+1}^n b_j e_j$. Modulo $F$, the first sum vanishes, so $[v]=\sum_{j=k+1}^n b_j [e_j]$. Thus the classes $[e_{k+1}],\ldots,[e_n]$ span $E/F$.

**Independence.** Suppose $\sum_{j=k+1}^n b_j [e_j]=0$. Then $\sum b_j e_j\in F$, so it is a combination of the $e_1,\ldots,e_k$. By independence of the full basis, all $b_j=0$.

**Conclusion.** $([e_{k+1}],\ldots,[e_n])$ is a basis of $E/F$, so $\dim(E/F)=n-k=\dim E-\dim F$.

@@@ vector-dual-basis.md
---
title: The dual basis
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: dual space, dual basis, linear forms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{B}=(e_1,e_2,e_3)$ be a basis of $\mathbb{R}^3$. Define the dual basis $(\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3)$ by $\varphi_i(e_j)=\delta_{ij}$. Show that $(\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3)$ is a basis of the dual space $E^*$.

## Hint

Show independence: if $\sum\lambda_i\varphi_i=0$, evaluate at each $e_j$. Show spanning: any $\varphi$ equals $\sum\varphi(e_i)\varphi_i$.

## Solution

**Independence.** Suppose $\sum_{i=1}^3\lambda_i\varphi_i=0$ (the zero linear form). Evaluating at $e_j$:
$$0=\left(\sum_i\lambda_i\varphi_i\right)(e_j)=\sum_i\lambda_i\varphi_i(e_j)=\lambda_j.$$
So all $\lambda_j=0$, and the family is independent.

**Spanning.** Let $\varphi\in E^*$. Define $\psi=\sum_{i=1}^3\varphi(e_i)\varphi_i$. For each $j$,
$$\psi(e_j)=\sum_i\varphi(e_i)\varphi_i(e_j)=\varphi(e_j).$$
So $\psi$ and $\varphi$ agree on the basis, hence $\psi=\varphi$.

**Conclusion.** $(\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\varphi_3)$ is a basis of $E^*$, called the dual basis of $\mathcal{B}$.

@@@ vector-transpose-kernel.md
---
title: Kernel of the transpose
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: transpose, kernel, orthogonal complement, rank
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $m\times n$ real matrix. Show that $\ker(A^T)=(\operatorname{Im} A)^\perp$ in $\mathbb{R}^m$.

## Hint

$y\in\ker(A^T)$ iff $A^T y=0$ iff $y\cdot Ax=0$ for all $x$.

## Solution

**Equivalence.** Let $y\in\mathbb{R}^m$. Then
$$y\in\ker(A^T)\iff A^T y=0\iff \text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^n,\ (A^T y)\cdot x=0.$$
But $(A^T y)\cdot x = y\cdot(Ax)$, so this is equivalent to $y\cdot(Ax)=0$ for all $x$, i.e. $y\perp\operatorname{Im} A$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker(A^T)=(\operatorname{Im} A)^\perp$.

**Consequence.** $\operatorname{rank}(A^T)=m-\dim\ker(A^T)=m-\dim(\operatorname{Im} A)^\perp=m-(m-\operatorname{rank}A)=\operatorname{rank}A$. So row rank equals column rank.

@@@ vector-subspace-generated-matrix.md
---
title: Subspace generated by columns of a matrix
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: column space, generators, rank
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 3 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Find a basis of the column space of $A$.

## Hint

Column-reduce or transpose and row-reduce; the pivot columns of $A$ give a basis.

## Solution

**Observation.** Column 3 = column 1 + column 2, so column 3 is redundant.

**Independence of columns 1 and 2.** The two columns $(1,0,1)$ and $(2,1,3)$ are not proportional, so they are independent.

**Conclusion.** A basis of the column space is $((1,0,1),(2,1,3))$, and the rank is $2$.

**Check.** Row reduction confirms rank $2$: $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1-L_2$ gives the zero row.

@@@ vector-dimension-hyperplane.md
---
title: Hyperplanes and linear forms
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: hyperplane, linear form, kernel, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional vector space of dimension $n\ge1$. Show that a subspace $H$ is a hyperplane (i.e. $\dim H=n-1$) if and only if there exists a nonzero linear form $\varphi\in E^*$ with $H=\ker\varphi$.

## Hint

For the forward direction, take a basis of $H$, extend it by one vector, and define $\varphi$ to vanish on $H$ and be $1$ on the extra vector.

## Solution

**If $H=\ker\varphi$ with $\varphi\neq0$.** Then $\varphi$ is surjective (nonzero linear form onto $\mathbb{R}$), so by rank-nullity, $\dim\ker\varphi=n-1$. So $H$ is a hyperplane.

**Conversely, assume $\dim H=n-1$.** Choose a basis $(e_1,\ldots,e_{n-1})$ of $H$, and complete it with $e_n$ to a basis of $E$. Define $\varphi$ to be the linear form with $\varphi(e_i)=0$ for $i\le n-1$ and $\varphi(e_n)=1$. Then $\varphi$ is a nonzero linear form with kernel exactly $H$ (vectors with zero $e_n$-coordinate).

**Conclusion.** Hyperplanes are exactly the kernels of nonzero linear forms.

@@@ vector-intersection-kernels.md
---
title: Intersection of two kernels
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: intersection, kernel, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f,g:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}$ be the linear forms $f(x,y,z)=x+y+z$ and $g(x,y,z)=x-y$. Compute $\dim(\ker f\cap\ker g)$.

## Hint

The intersection is the kernel of the map $(f,g):\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Solution

**Map.** $(f,g)(x,y,z)=(x+y+z,x-y)$.

**Matrix.**
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The two rows are not proportional, so the rank is $2$.

**Dimension.** $\dim(\ker f\cap\ker g)=3-2=1$.

**Explicit.** Solve $x+y+z=0$ and $x=y$: $2x+z=0$, so $z=-2x$. Kernel is spanned by $(1,1,-2)$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker f\cap\ker g$ is the line spanned by $(1,1,-2)$.

@@@ vector-basis-four-dim.md
---
title: A basis of a hyperplane in $\mathbb{R}^4$
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: basis, hyperplane, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find a basis of the hyperplane $H=\{(x,y,z,t)\in\mathbb{R}^4 : x+2y+3z+4t=0\}$.

## Hint

The hyperplane is the kernel of a nonzero linear form. Solve the equation with three free parameters.

## Solution

**Solve.** $x=-2y-3z-4t$. The solutions are
$$(x,y,z,t)=(-2y-3z-4t, y, z, t) = y(-2,1,0,0)+z(-3,0,1,0)+t(-4,0,0,1).$$

**Basis.** $((-2,1,0,0),(-3,0,1,0),(-4,0,0,1))$.

**Independence.** The three vectors are independent (their second, third, fourth coordinates form the identity).

**Conclusion.** $\dim H=3$ and the family above is a basis.

@@@ vector-eigenvectors-subspace.md
---
title: Eigenspaces sum to the whole space
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: eigenspaces, direct sum, diagonalization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix with distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r$. Show that the eigenspaces $E_{\lambda_1},\ldots,E_{\lambda_r}$ are in direct sum.

## Hint

Show that a linear combination of eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues that equals zero must have all coefficients zero, by induction.

## Solution

**Induction on $r$.** For $r=1$ there is nothing to prove. Suppose the result holds for $r-1$ eigenvalues. Let $v_i\in E_{\lambda_i}$ with $\sum_{i=1}^r v_i=0$. Applying $A$:
$$\sum_{i=1}^r \lambda_i v_i=0.$$
Subtracting $\lambda_r$ times the first relation:
$$\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(\lambda_i-\lambda_r)v_i=0.$$
By the induction hypothesis applied to $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{r-1}$, each $(\lambda_i-\lambda_r)v_i=0$. Since $\lambda_i\neq\lambda_r$, $v_i=0$ for $i<r$. Then $v_r=0$ as well.

**Conclusion.** The eigenspaces are in direct sum, and $\sum_i\dim E_{\lambda_i}\le n$.

@@@ vector-complement-existence.md
---
title: Existence of a supplementary subspace
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: supplementary subspace, basis extension, existence
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F$ be a subspace of a finite-dimensional vector space $E$. Show that there exists a subspace $G$ of $E$ such that $E=F\oplus G$.

## Hint

Take a basis of $F$, extend it to a basis of $E$, and let $G$ be the span of the added vectors.

## Solution

**Basis extension.** Let $(e_1,\ldots,e_k)$ be a basis of $F$. By the incomplete basis theorem, it extends to a basis $(e_1,\ldots,e_k,e_{k+1},\ldots,e_n)$ of $E$.

**Define $G$.** Let $G=\operatorname{span}(e_{k+1},\ldots,e_n)$.

**Direct sum.** Any $v\in E$ is a combination of the full basis, hence $v=v_F+v_G$ with $v_F\in F$ and $v_G\in G$. If $v\in F\cap G$, then $v$ is a combination of the $e_i$ for $i\le k$ and of the $e_j$ for $j>k$; by uniqueness of coordinates in the basis, all coefficients are zero, so $v=0$.

**Conclusion.** $E=F\oplus G$. Any subspace has at least one supplementary subspace.

@@@ vector-linear-map-addition.md
---
title: The space of linear maps
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: linear maps, vector space structure, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $E$ and $F$ be finite-dimensional vector spaces, with $\dim E=n$ and $\dim F=m$. Show that $\mathcal{L}(E,F)$ is a vector space of dimension $mn$.

## Hint

Fix bases of $E$ and $F$; the map $f\mapsto$ matrix of $f$ is a linear isomorphism onto $\mathcal{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})$.

## Solution

**Vector space structure.** For $f,g\in\mathcal{L}(E,F)$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$, define $(f+g)(x)=f(x)+g(x)$ and $(\lambda f)(x)=\lambda f(x)$. These are linear maps, and the axioms hold pointwise.

**Isomorphism.** Fix a basis $\mathcal{B}$ of $E$ and a basis $\mathcal{C}$ of $F$. The map $\Phi:f\mapsto[f]_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{C}}$ (matrix of $f$ in these bases) is linear and bijective, with inverse $M\mapsto$ the linear map with matrix $M$.

**Dimension.** $\dim\mathcal{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})=mn$, so $\dim\mathcal{L}(E,F)=mn$.

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal{L}(E,F)$ is a vector space of dimension $mn$.

@@@ linear-map-definition-check.md
---
title: Checking linearity
category: linear/linear-maps
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: linear map, definition, examples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine whether each map is linear:

(a) $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$, $f(x,y)=(2x-y, x+3y)$;
(b) $g:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}$, $g(x,y)=xy$;
(c) $h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$, $h(x)=x+1$;
(d) $k:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$, $k(x,y)=(x^2,y)$.

## Hint

Check $f(u+v)=f(u)+f(v)$ and $f(\lambda u)=\lambda f(u)$. A quick necessary condition is $f(0)=0$.

## Solution

**(a)** $f(\lambda(x,y))=(2\lambda x-\lambda y,\lambda x+3\lambda y)=\lambda f(x,y)$, and $f((x,y)+(x',y'))=(2(x+x')-(y+y'),(x+x')+3(y+y'))=f(x,y)+f(x',y')$. Linear.

**(b)** $g(2,3)=6$ but $2g(1,1)+g(0,1)=2\cdot1+0=2\neq6$; alternatively $g(\lambda x,\lambda y)=\lambda^2 xy\neq\lambda g(x,y)$ in general. Not linear.

**(c)** $h(0)=1\neq0$. Not linear.

**(d)** $k(2,1)=(4,1)$ but $2k(1,1)=(2,2)$. Not linear.

**Conclusion.** Only (a) is linear.

@@@ linear-map-matrix.md
---
title: Matrix of a linear map
category: linear/linear-maps
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: matrix representation, canonical basis, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be defined by $f(x,y,z)=(x+2y, 3y-z)$. Write the matrix of $f$ in the canonical bases.

## Hint

The columns are the images of the canonical basis vectors.

## Solution

**Images.**
$$f(1,0,0)=(1,0), \quad f(0,1,0)=(2,3), \quad f(0,0,1)=(0,-1).$$

**Matrix.** These are the columns:
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Check.** $A(x,y,z)^T = (x+2y, 3y-z)^T$, consistent with $f$.

@@@ linear-map-kernel-basic.md
---
title: Computing a kernel
category: linear/linear-maps
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: kernel, linear map, basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be defined by $f(x,y,z)=(x+y, y+z, x+2y+z)$. Compute $\ker f$.

## Hint

Solve $f(x,y,z)=(0,0,0)$.

## Solution

**System.**
$$x+y=0, \qquad y+z=0, \qquad x+2y+z=0.$$
From the first, $x=-y$. From the second, $z=-y$. The third then becomes $-y+2y-y=0$, automatically satisfied.

**Kernel.** $\ker f=\{( -y, y, -y): y\in\mathbb{R}\}=\operatorname{span}((-1,1,-1))$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker f$ is the line spanned by $(-1,1,-1)$.

@@@ linear-map-image-basic.md
---
title: Computing an image
category: linear/linear-maps
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: image, linear map, generators
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be defined by $f(x,y)=(x+y, x-y, 2x)$. Compute $\operatorname{Im} f$ and its dimension.

## Hint

The image is spanned by the images of a basis of $\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Solution

**Generators.** $f(1,0)=(1,1,2)$ and $f(0,1)=(1,-1,0)$.

**Independence.** The two vectors are not proportional, so they are independent.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{Im} f=\operatorname{span}((1,1,2),(1,-1,0))$, a plane in $\mathbb{R}^3$, of dimension $2$.

@@@ linear-map-rank-nullity.md
---
title: Rank-nullity theorem on an example
category: linear/linear-maps
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: rank-nullity, rank, kernel dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^4\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be the linear map with matrix
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 1 \\ 2 & 4 & 6 & 3 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute $\operatorname{rank}(f)$ and $\dim\ker f$, and check the rank-nullity theorem.

## Hint

Row-reduce $A$ and count nonzero rows.

## Solution

**Row reduction.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-2L_1$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
$L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_2$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Rank is $2$.

**Kernel dimension.** By rank-nullity, $\dim\ker f=4-2=2$.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{rank}(f)=2$ and $\dim\ker f=2$, with $2+2=4$.

@@@ linear-map-injective.md
---
title: An injective linear map
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: injectivity, kernel, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be defined by $f(x,y)=(x+y, x-y, 2x)$. Show that $f$ is injective.

## Hint

Compute $\ker f$.

## Solution

**Kernel.** Solve $x+y=0$, $x-y=0$, $2x=0$. From the first two, adding gives $2x=0$, so $x=0$, then $y=0$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker f=\{0\}$, so $f$ is injective. Since $\dim\mathbb{R}^2=2$ and $f$ is injective, $\operatorname{Im} f$ has dimension $2$, consistent with the earlier computation.

@@@ linear-map-surjective.md
---
title: A surjective linear map
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: surjectivity, rank, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be defined by $f(x,y,z)=(x+y, y+z)$. Show that $f$ is surjective.

## Hint

Find preimages of the standard basis of $\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Solution

**Preimages.** $f(1,0,0)=(1,0)$ and $f(0,0,1)=(0,1)$.

**Surjectivity.** For any $(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^2$, $f(a,0,b)=(a,b)$.

**Kernel.** $x+y=0$, $y+z=0$ gives $y=-x$, $z=x$, so $\ker f=\operatorname{span}((1,-1,1))$.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is surjective, $\operatorname{rank}(f)=2$, $\dim\ker f=1$, and $2+1=3$.

@@@ linear-map-isomorphism.md
---
title: An isomorphism
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: isomorphism, inverse, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be defined by $f(x,y)=(2x+y, x-y)$. Show that $f$ is an isomorphism and compute $f^{-1}$.

## Hint

Show that $\ker f=\{0\}$, or compute the inverse of the matrix.

## Solution

**Matrix.**
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad \det A = -2-1=-3\neq0.$$
So $A$ is invertible and $f$ is an isomorphism.

**Inverse.**
$$A^{-1} = -\frac13\begin{pmatrix} -1 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \frac13\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 \end{pmatrix}.$$
So $f^{-1}(u,v)=\frac13(u+v, u-2v)$.

**Verification.** $f(f^{-1}(u,v))=(u,v)$:
$$f\left(\frac{u+v}{3}, \frac{u-2v}{3}\right) = \left(\frac{2(u+v)+(u-2v)}{3}, \frac{(u+v)-(u-2v)}{3}\right)=(u,v).$$

@@@ linear-map-rank-basic.md
---
title: Rank of a linear map
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, image dimension, matrix
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be defined by
$$f(x,y,z)=(x+2y, 2x+4y, 3x+6y+z).$$
Compute $\operatorname{rank}(f)$.

## Hint

Write the matrix and row-reduce.

## Solution

**Matrix.**
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 2 & 4 & 0 \\ 3 & 6 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Row reduction.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-2L_1$ gives zero row; $L_3\leftarrow L_3-3L_1$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Rank is $2$.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{rank}(f)=2$, $\dim\ker f=1$.

@@@ linear-map-composition.md
---
title: Composition of linear maps
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: composition, matrix product, linear maps
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^3$ have matrix
$$A=\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix},$$
and $g:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^2$ have matrix
$$B=\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute the matrix of $g\circ f$.

## Hint

The matrix of $g\circ f$ is $BA$.

## Solution

**Product.**
$$BA = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 1+0+1 & 2+0+0 \\ 2+0+0 & 4+1+0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 2 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** The matrix of $g\circ f$ is $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 2 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$.

@@@ linear-map-kernel-composition.md
---
title: Kernel of a composition
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: composition, kernel, inclusion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to F$ and $g:F\to G$ be linear maps. Show that $\ker f\subseteq\ker(g\circ f)$, with equality if $g$ is injective on $\operatorname{Im} f$.

## Hint

For the first inclusion, use $f(x)=0\implies g(f(x))=0$. For equality, suppose $g(f(x))=0$ and use injectivity.

## Solution

**Inclusion.** If $x\in\ker f$, then $f(x)=0$, so $(g\circ f)(x)=g(0)=0$, i.e. $x\in\ker(g\circ f)$.

**Equality when $g$ is injective on $\operatorname{Im} f$.** Suppose $x\in\ker(g\circ f)$. Then $g(f(x))=0$. Since $f(x)\in\operatorname{Im} f$ and $g$ is injective on this set, and since $g(0)=0$, we get $f(x)=0$. So $x\in\ker f$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker f\subseteq\ker(g\circ f)$, with equality under the stated condition.

@@@ linear-map-image-composition.md
---
title: Image of a composition
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: composition, image, inclusion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to F$ and $g:F\to G$ be linear maps. Show that $\operatorname{Im}(g\circ f)\subseteq\operatorname{Im} g$, with equality if $g$ is surjective.

## Hint

The image of $g\circ f$ is $g(\operatorname{Im} f)$.

## Solution

**Inclusion.** $\operatorname{Im}(g\circ f)=g(\operatorname{Im} f)\subseteq g(F)=\operatorname{Im} g$.

**Equality if $g$ surjective.** If $\operatorname{Im} g=G$, then for every $w\in G$ there is $y\in F$ with $g(y)=w$. But $y$ itself may not be in $\operatorname{Im} f$. So equality requires more than surjectivity of $g$ in general; a sufficient condition is that $\operatorname{Im} f+\ker g=F$. Under this condition, write $y=y_f+k$ with $y_f\in\operatorname{Im} f$ and $k\in\ker g$; then $g(y)=g(y_f)$, so $w\in g(\operatorname{Im} f)$.

**Conclusion.** In general $\operatorname{Im}(g\circ f)\subseteq\operatorname{Im} g$, with equality when $\operatorname{Im} f+\ker g=F$ (in particular when $g$ is injective, or when $\operatorname{Im} f=F$).

@@@ linear-map-matrix-composition.md
---
title: Composition and matrix product
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: composition, matrix product, representation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^m$ and $g:\mathbb{R}^m\to\mathbb{R}^p$ be linear maps with matrices $A$ and $B$ in the canonical bases. Show that the matrix of $g\circ f$ is $BA$.

## Hint

Compute $g(f(x))$ using the matrix product.

## Solution

**Matrix action.** For $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$, $f(x)=Ax\in\mathbb{R}^m$ and $g(y)=By\in\mathbb{R}^p$. Therefore
$$(g\circ f)(x)=g(f(x))=B(Ax)=(BA)x.$$
So the linear map $g\circ f$ is represented by the matrix $BA$.

**Conclusion.** Composition of linear maps corresponds to matrix multiplication (in the same order).

@@@ linear-map-change-of-basis.md
---
title: Change of basis for a linear map
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: change of basis, matrix representation, similarity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ have matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$ in the canonical basis. Compute the matrix of $f$ in the basis $\mathcal{B}=((1,1),(1,-1))$.

## Hint

If $P$ is the transition matrix from the canonical basis to $\mathcal{B}$, the new matrix is $P^{-1}AP$.

## Solution

**Transition matrix.**
$$P = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad P^{-1} = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**New matrix.**
$$P^{-1}AP = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 3 & -1 \end{pmatrix},$$
then
$$\frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 3 & -1 \end{pmatrix} = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 6 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** In $\mathcal{B}$, the matrix is $\operatorname{diag}(3,1)$.

@@@ linear-map-similarity-properties.md
---
title: Invariants under similarity
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: similarity, trace, determinant, rank
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ and $B$ be similar matrices. Show that $A$ and $B$ have the same rank, trace, and determinant.

## Hint

$B=P^{-1}AP$. For the rank, use that $P$ and $P^{-1}$ are isomorphisms. For trace and determinant, use the properties of the trace and the multiplicativity of the determinant.

## Solution

**Rank.** Multiplication by an invertible matrix preserves the rank (it is an isomorphism). So $\operatorname{rank}(B)=\operatorname{rank}(P^{-1}AP)=\operatorname{rank}(A)$.

**Trace.** Using the cyclic property: $\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}AP)=\operatorname{tr}(APP^{-1})=\operatorname{tr}(A)$.

**Determinant.**
$$\det B = \det(P^{-1}AP)=\det(P^{-1})\det A\det P=\det A.$$

**Conclusion.** Rank, trace, and determinant are all invariant under similarity. In particular, the characteristic polynomial is also invariant.

@@@ linear-map-diagonalization-criterion.md
---
title: Diagonalizability and the sum of eigenspaces
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalization, eigenspaces, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to E$ be a linear endomorphism of a finite-dimensional space $E$. Show that $f$ is diagonalizable if and only if $\sum_\lambda\dim E_\lambda=\dim E$, where $E_\lambda=\ker(f-\lambda\,\mathrm{id})$.

## Hint

If $f$ is diagonalizable, there is a basis of eigenvectors. Conversely, if the sum of dimensions equals $\dim E$, the union of bases of the eigenspaces is a basis of $E$.

## Solution

**Forward direction.** If $f$ is diagonalizable, there is a basis of eigenvectors. Each eigenvector lies in some eigenspace, so the sum of the dimensions of the eigenspaces is at least $\dim E$. Since the eigenspaces are in direct sum (distinct eigenvalues), their dimensions sum to exactly $\dim E$.

**Converse.** If the sum of the dimensions equals $\dim E$, choose a basis of each eigenspace. Because the eigenspaces are in direct sum, the union of these bases has $\dim E$ vectors and is linearly independent, hence a basis of $E$. This basis consists of eigenvectors, so $f$ is diagonalizable.

**Conclusion.** Diagonalizability is equivalent to the sum of the eigenspace dimensions being $\dim E$.

@@@ linear-map-kernel-basis-3x3.md
---
title: Basis of a kernel
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: kernel, basis, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^3$ have matrix
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 2 & 4 \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Find a basis of $\ker f$.

## Hint

Row-reduce $A$ and solve $Ax=0$.

## Solution

**Row reduction.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-2L_1$ gives zero row; $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Swapping rows:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 2 \\ 0 & -1 & -1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Rank is $2$, $\dim\ker f=1$.

**Solve.** From $-y-z=0$, $y=-z$. From $x+y+2z=0$, $x+z-2z=0$?? Wait: $x+y+2z=0$ with $y=-z$ gives $x-z+2z=0$, i.e. $x=-z$. So $(x,y,z)=(-z,-z,z)=z(-1,-1,1)$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker f=\operatorname{span}((-1,-1,1))$.

@@@ linear-map-rank-inequality.md
---
title: Rank inequality for a composition
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: rank, composition, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to F$ and $g:F\to G$ be linear maps. Show that
$$\operatorname{rank}(g\circ f)\ge\operatorname{rank}(f)+\operatorname{rank}(g)-\dim F.$$

## Hint

Apply rank-nullity to $g$ restricted to $\operatorname{Im} f$, or use the inclusion $\ker(g\circ f)\subseteq f^{-1}(\ker g)$.

## Solution

**Kernel inclusion.** $\ker(g\circ f)=f^{-1}(\ker g)$. Therefore
$$\dim\ker(g\circ f)\le\dim\ker f+\dim\ker g.$$
(Indeed, the restriction of $f$ to $f^{-1}(\ker g)$ has kernel $\ker f$ and image in $\ker g$.)

**Rank-nullity.** $\operatorname{rank}(g\circ f)=\dim E-\dim\ker(g\circ f)$, $\operatorname{rank}(f)=\dim E-\dim\ker f$, $\operatorname{rank}(g)=\dim F-\dim\ker g$.

**Inequality.** Combining:
$$\operatorname{rank}(g\circ f)\ge\dim E-\dim\ker f-\dim\ker g=\operatorname{rank}(f)+\operatorname{rank}(g)-\dim F.$$

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds; it is known as **Sylvester's rank inequality**.

@@@ linear-map-projection.md
---
title: A linear projection
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: projection, idempotence, kernel and image
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $p:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be defined by $p(x,y)=(x,0)$. Show that $p$ is linear, compute $\ker p$ and $\operatorname{Im} p$, and verify $p\circ p=p$.

## Hint

Project onto the first coordinate.

## Solution

**Linearity.** $p$ is a linear map (it is the linear form $(x,y)\mapsto x$ applied to a coordinate). Direct check: $p(\lambda u+\mu v)=\lambda p(u)+\mu p(v)$.

**Kernel.** $p(x,y)=0\iff x=0$, so $\ker p=\{(0,y): y\in\mathbb{R}\}=\operatorname{span}((0,1))$, the $y$-axis.

**Image.** $p(x,y)=(x,0)$, so $\operatorname{Im} p=\{(x,0): x\in\mathbb{R}\}=\operatorname{span}((1,0))$, the $x$-axis.

**Idempotence.** $p(p(x,y))=p(x,0)=(x,0)=p(x,y)$.

**Conclusion.** $p$ is a projection onto the $x$-axis, parallel to the $y$-axis.

@@@ linear-map-symmetry.md
---
title: A linear symmetry
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: symmetry, involution, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $s:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be defined by $s(x,y)=(y,x)$. Show that $s$ is linear and $s\circ s=\mathrm{id}$.

## Hint

Direct check.

## Solution

**Linearity.** $s(\lambda(x,y))=( \lambda y,\lambda x)=\lambda(y,x)=\lambda s(x,y)$, and $s((x,y)+(x',y'))=(y+y',x+x')=s(x,y)+s(x',y')$.

**Involution.** $s(s(x,y))=s(y,x)=(x,y)$, so $s^2=\mathrm{id}$.

**Eigenvalues and eigenspaces.** $\lambda=1$: $y=x$, eigenspace the line $(1,1)$. $\lambda=-1$: $y=-x$, eigenspace the line $(1,-1)$. The two lines are in direct sum, and $s$ is the reflection across the line $y=x$.

**Conclusion.** $s$ is a linear involution (a symmetry), and it is diagonalizable with eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$.

@@@ linear-map-nilpotent.md
---
title: A nilpotent endomorphism
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: nilpotent, kernel, image
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be defined by $f(x,y,z)=(y,z,0)$. Show that $f$ is linear, compute $f^2$ and $f^3$, and describe $\ker f$ and $\operatorname{Im} f$.

## Hint

Apply $f$ repeatedly.

## Solution

**Linearity.** $f$ is linear (each coordinate is linear).

**Powers.** $f^2(x,y,z)=f(y,z,0)=(z,0,0)$, and $f^3(x,y,z)=f(z,0,0)=(0,0,0)$. So $f^3=0$: $f$ is nilpotent of index $3$.

**Kernel.** $f(x,y,z)=0$ iff $y=z=0$, so $\ker f=\{(x,0,0)\}=\operatorname{span}((1,0,0))$, dimension $1$.

**Image.** $f(x,y,z)=(y,z,0)$, so $\operatorname{Im} f=\{(y,z,0)\}=\operatorname{span}((1,0,0),(0,1,0))$, dimension $2$.

**Check.** $\dim\ker f+\dim\operatorname{Im} f=1+2=3$.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is nilpotent of index $3$, with $\ker f\subseteq\operatorname{Im} f$ (both contain the $x$-axis).

@@@ linear-map-kernel-image-sum.md
---
title: Kernel and image of an endomorphism
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: kernel, image, direct sum, endomorphism
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to E$ be a linear endomorphism of a finite-dimensional space. Show that $f$ is invertible if and only if $E=\ker f\oplus\operatorname{Im} f$.

## Hint

If $f$ is invertible, $\ker f=\{0\}$ and $\operatorname{Im} f=E$. Conversely, if $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f=\{0\}$ and the dimensions sum to $\dim E$, then $\ker f=\{0\}$.

## Solution

**If $f$ is invertible.** Then $\ker f=\{0\}$ and $\operatorname{Im} f=E$, so the sum is trivially direct and equal to $E$.

**Conversely, assume $E=\ker f\oplus\operatorname{Im} f$.** Then $\dim\ker f+\dim\operatorname{Im} f=\dim E$ (this holds for any linear map by rank-nullity, so the direct-sum hypothesis adds only $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f=\{0\}$). Suppose $x\in\ker f$. Then $x=f(y)$ for some $y$ (since $x\in E=\operatorname{Im} f+\ker f$ and $x\in\ker f$ means we can write $x=x_k+x_i$; but $x\in\ker f$ and $x\in\operatorname{Im} f+\ker f$: write $x=x'$ with $x'\in\operatorname{Im} f$ by taking $x_i=x$, $x_k=0$; wait, we need a cleaner argument).

Cleaner: if $x\in\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f$, then $x=f(y)$ for some $y$ and $f(x)=0$. Then $f^2(y)=0$. If $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f=\{0\}$, then $f(y)\in\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f$, so $f(y)=0$, i.e. $y\in\ker f$, i.e. $x=f(y)=0$. Hence $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f=\{0\}$ forces: $x=f(y)$ and $f(x)=0$ implies $f^2(y)=0$, but that alone doesn't imply $f(y)=0$ unless $f^2=f\circ f$ has kernel containing $\operatorname{Im} f$... 

Actually the correct statement is: if $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f=\{0\}$, then $f$ restricted to $\operatorname{Im} f$ is injective (since its kernel is $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f$). But that does not directly give $f$ invertible.

Let me restate: the equivalence "$f$ invertible $\iff$ $E=\ker f\oplus\operatorname{Im} f$" is **false** in general. For example, the nilpotent $f(x,y,z)=(y,z,0)$ has $\ker f\cap\operatorname{Im} f=\{(x,0,0)\}\neq\{0\}$, and indeed $f$ is not invertible; but for a suitable map, $\ker f\oplus\operatorname{Im} f$ could be $E$ without $f$ being invertible (e.g. on $\mathbb{R}^2$, $f(x,y)=(x,0)$: $\ker f=\{(0,y)\}$, $\operatorname{Im} f=\{(x,0)\}$, and $\ker f\oplus\operatorname{Im} f=\mathbb{R}^2$, but $f$ is not invertible).

**Conclusion.** The proposed equivalence is **false**. The correct equivalence is: $f$ is invertible iff $\ker f=\{0\}$ (equivalently $\operatorname{Im} f=E$). The direct-sum condition $E=\ker f\oplus\operatorname{Im} f$ holds for many non-invertible $f$ (e.g. any projection).

@@@ linear-map-nonzero-eigenvalue.md
---
title: Eigenvectors and injectivity
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenvectors, injectivity, kernel
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to E$ be a linear endomorphism. Show that $f$ is injective if and only if $0$ is not an eigenvalue of $f$.

## Hint

$0$ is an eigenvalue iff there is a nonzero $v$ with $f(v)=0$, i.e. $\ker f\neq\{0\}$.

## Solution

**$0$ eigenvalue $\iff\ker f\neq\{0\}$.** $0$ is an eigenvalue of $f$ iff there exists $v\neq 0$ with $f(v)=0\cdot v=0$, iff $\ker f\neq\{0\}$.

**Conclusion.** By the injectivity criterion ($f$ injective $\iff\ker f=\{0\}$), $f$ is injective iff $0$ is not an eigenvalue.

@@@ linear-map-matrix-rank-invariance.md
---
title: Rank is invariant under invertible multiplications
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, invertible matrices, invariance
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A\in\mathcal{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})$, $P\in GL_m(\mathbb{R})$, $Q\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$. Show that
$$\operatorname{rank}(PA)=\operatorname{rank}(A), \qquad \operatorname{rank}(AQ)=\operatorname{rank}(A), \qquad \operatorname{rank}(PAQ)=\operatorname{rank}(A).$$

## Hint

Multiplication by an invertible matrix is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

## Solution

**$PA$.** Multiplication by $P$ is an isomorphism $\mathbb{R}^m\to\mathbb{R}^m$, so it preserves dimensions of subspaces. Thus
$$\operatorname{rank}(PA)=\dim(P(\operatorname{Im} A))=\dim(\operatorname{Im} A)=\operatorname{rank}(A).$$

**$AQ$.** Multiplication by $Q$ is an isomorphism $\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$, so $\operatorname{Im}(AQ)=A(\operatorname{Im} Q)=A(\mathbb{R}^n)=\operatorname{Im} A$. Thus $\operatorname{rank}(AQ)=\operatorname{rank}(A)$.

**$PAQ$.** Combine: $\operatorname{rank}(PAQ)=\operatorname{rank}(AQ)=\operatorname{rank}(A)$.

**Conclusion.** The rank is invariant under left and right multiplication by invertible matrices.

@@@ linear-map-orthogonal-projection.md
---
title: Projection onto a subspace
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: orthogonal projection, linear map, kernel and image
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ be a subspace with orthonormal basis $(u_1,\ldots,u_k)$. Define $p(x)=\sum_{i=1}^k\langle x,u_i\rangle u_i$. Show that $p$ is linear, compute $\ker p$ and $\operatorname{Im} p$, and check $p^2=p$.

## Hint

Linearity of $p$ comes from linearity of the inner product. For the kernel, use the orthogonal complement.

## Solution

**Linearity.** For $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$p(x+\lambda y)=\sum_i\langle x+\lambda y,u_i\rangle u_i=\sum_i(\langle x,u_i\rangle+\lambda\langle y,u_i\rangle)u_i=p(x)+\lambda p(y).$$
So $p$ is linear.

**Image.** $p(x)$ is a combination of the $u_i$, so $\operatorname{Im} p\subseteq F$. Conversely, if $x\in F$, then $x=\sum_i\langle x,u_i\rangle u_i=p(x)$, so $F\subseteq\operatorname{Im} p$. Therefore $\operatorname{Im} p=F$.

**Kernel.** $p(x)=0$ iff $x$ is orthogonal to every $u_i$, iff $x\in F^\perp$. So $\ker p=F^\perp$.

**Idempotence.** For $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$, $p(x)\in F$, and $p$ is the identity on $F$ (as shown above), so $p(p(x))=p(x)$.

**Conclusion.** $p$ is the orthogonal projection onto $F$, with $\ker p=F^\perp$, $\operatorname{Im} p=F$, and $p^2=p$.

@@@ linear-map-endomorphism-inverse.md
---
title: Inverse of an endomorphism
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: inverse, endomorphism, matrix inverse
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^3$ have matrix
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 0 & 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Show that $f$ is an isomorphism and compute $f^{-1}(x,y,z)$.

## Hint

$A$ is upper triangular with nonzero diagonal, hence invertible. Compute $A^{-1}$ by back-substitution.

## Solution

**Invertibility.** $\det A = 1\cdot1\cdot1=1\neq0$, so $A$ is invertible and $f$ is an isomorphism.

**Inverse.** Solve $A(x',y',z')=(x,y,z)$:
$$x'+2y'+3z'=x, \quad y'+4z'=y, \quad z'=z.$$
Back-substituting: $z'=z$; $y'=y-4z$; $x'=x-2(y-4z)-3z=x-2y+5z$.

**Conclusion.** $f^{-1}(x,y,z)=(x-2y+5z,\ y-4z,\ z)$.

@@@ linear-map-matrix-kernel-image-relation.md
---
title: Kernel and image of $A$ and $A^T$
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: kernel, image, transpose, orthogonal complement
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A\in\mathcal{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})$. Show that
$$\ker A=(\operatorname{Im} A^T)^\perp, \qquad \operatorname{Im} A=(\ker A^T)^\perp,$$
where the orthogonal complements are taken in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and $\mathbb{R}^m$ respectively.

## Hint

Use the identity $\langle Ax,y\rangle=\langle x,A^T y\rangle$.

## Solution

**First identity.** Let $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$. Then
$$x\in\ker A\iff Ax=0\iff \langle Ax,y\rangle=0\ \forall y\in\mathbb{R}^m\iff \langle x,A^T y\rangle=0\ \forall y\in\mathbb{R}^m.$$
So $x$ is orthogonal to every vector of the form $A^T y$, i.e. to $\operatorname{Im} A^T$. Hence $\ker A=(\operatorname{Im} A^T)^\perp$.

**Second identity.** Apply the first identity to $A^T$: $\ker A^T=(\operatorname{Im} A)^\perp$. Taking orthogonal complements: $(\ker A^T)^\perp=\operatorname{Im} A$.

**Conclusion.** Both identities hold. They imply $\operatorname{rank} A+\dim\ker A^T=m$ and $\operatorname{rank} A+\dim\ker A=n$ (rank-nullity), which is consistent with $\operatorname{rank} A=\operatorname{rank} A^T$.

@@@ linear-map-invariant-subspace.md
---
title: Invariant subspaces
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: invariant subspace, endomorphism, restriction
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to E$ be a linear endomorphism, and $F$ a subspace of $E$. We say $F$ is $f$-invariant if $f(F)\subseteq F$. Show that if $F$ is $f$-invariant, the restriction $f|_F:F\to F$ is a well-defined linear map.

## Hint

For $x\in F$, $f|_F(x)=f(x)$ is in $F$ by invariance. Linearity is inherited from $f$.

## Solution

**Well-defined.** If $x\in F$, then $f(x)\in f(F)\subseteq F$, so $f|_F(x)\in F$.

**Linearity.** For $x,y\in F$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$: $f|_F(x+\lambda y)=f(x+\lambda y)=f(x)+\lambda f(y)=f|_F(x)+\lambda f|_F(y)$.

**Conclusion.** $f|_F$ is a well-defined linear endomorphism of $F$.

**Example.** If $f$ has an eigenvector $v$, the line $\operatorname{span}(v)$ is $f$-invariant. More generally, any eigenspace is $f$-invariant.

@@@ linear-map-quotient-induced.md
---
title: Induced map on a quotient
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: quotient space, induced map, well-defined
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:E\to E$ be a linear endomorphism and $F$ an $f$-invariant subspace. Show that $f$ induces a well-defined linear map $\bar f:E/F\to E/F$ by $\bar f([x])=[f(x)]$.

## Hint

Show that if $[x]=[y]$, then $[f(x)]=[f(y)]$.

## Solution

**Well-defined.** Suppose $[x]=[y]$. Then $x-y\in F$. Since $F$ is $f$-invariant, $f(x-y)\in F$, so $f(x)-f(y)\in F$, i.e. $[f(x)]=[f(y)]$. So $\bar f$ is well-defined.

**Linearity.** $\bar f([x]+\lambda[y])=\bar f([x+\lambda y])=[f(x+\lambda y)]=[f(x)+\lambda f(y)]=[f(x)]+\lambda[f(y)]=\bar f([x])+\lambda\bar f([y])$.

**Conclusion.** $\bar f$ is a well-defined linear endomorphism of $E/F$, called the **quotient map** induced by $f$.

**Remark.** This construction is the basis for studying the structure of endomorphisms via invariant subspaces.

@@@ eigenvalues-2x2-basic.md
---
title: Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: eigenvalues, eigenvectors, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

The matrix is upper triangular, so the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries. For each eigenvalue, solve $(A-\lambda I)v=0$.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues.** $A$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $3$ and $2$, so the eigenvalues are $\lambda_1=3$ and $\lambda_2=2$.

**Eigenvector for $\lambda=3$.**
$$A-3I = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The kernel is given by $y=0$, so an eigenvector is $v_1=(1,0)$.

**Eigenvector for $\lambda=2$.**
$$A-2I = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The kernel is given by $x+y=0$, so an eigenvector is $v_2=(1,-1)$.

**Conclusion.** Eigenvalues $3$ and $2$, with eigenvectors $(1,0)$ and $(1,-1)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-2x2-distinct.md
---
title: A 2Ã—2 matrix with distinct eigenvalues
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: eigenvalues, eigenvectors, distinct roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 1 \\ 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Compute the characteristic polynomial $\det(A-\lambda I)$ and find its roots.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\det(A-\lambda I) = \begin{vmatrix} 4-\lambda & 1 \\ 2 & 3-\lambda \end{vmatrix} = (4-\lambda)(3-\lambda)-2 = \lambda^2-7\lambda+10.$$
The roots are $\lambda_1=2$ and $\lambda_2=5$.

**Eigenvector for $\lambda=2$.**
$$A-2I = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The kernel is given by $2x+y=0$, so $v_1=(1,-2)$.

**Eigenvector for $\lambda=5$.**
$$A-5I = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 1 \\ 2 & -2 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The kernel is given by $-x+y=0$, so $v_2=(1,1)$.

**Conclusion.** Eigenvalues $2$ and $5$, with eigenvectors $(1,-2)$ and $(1,1)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-2x2-double.md
---
title: A 2Ã—2 matrix with a double eigenvalue
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenvalues, double root, diagonalizability
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Is $A$ diagonalizable?

## Hint

The characteristic polynomial has a double root at $2$. Compute the eigenspace $\ker(A-2I)$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\det(A-\lambda I) = (2-\lambda)^2.$$
So the only eigenvalue is $\lambda=2$, with algebraic multiplicity $2$.

**Eigenspace.**
$$A-2I = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The kernel is given by $y=0$, so $E_2=\operatorname{span}((1,0))$, dimension $1$.

**Conclusion.** The geometric multiplicity of $\lambda=2$ is $1<2$ (the algebraic multiplicity), so $A$ is **not diagonalizable**.

**Remark.** The matrix is a single Jordan block of size $2$ for the eigenvalue $2$.

@@@ eigenvalues-3x3-distinct.md
---
title: A 3Ã—3 matrix with distinct eigenvalues
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenvalues, eigenvectors, 3x3 matrices
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the eigenvalues of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad B = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Both matrices are triangular.

## Solution

**Matrix $A$.** $A$ is diagonal, so its eigenvalues are its diagonal entries: $1,2,3$.

**Matrix $B$.** $B$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $1,2,3$, so its eigenvalues are also $1,2,3$.

**Remark.** The eigenvalues of a triangular matrix are its diagonal entries. The two matrices $A$ and $B$ have the same eigenvalues but are not similar (the first is diagonalizable, the second is not, as we will see).

@@@ eigenvalues-3x3-symmetric.md
---
title: Eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: symmetric matrix, eigenvalues, trace and determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the eigenvalues of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Observe that the vector $(1,1,1)$ is an eigenvector, then use the trace and the determinant.

## Solution

**First eigenvalue.** $A(1,1,1)^T=(4,4,4)^T$, so $\lambda_1=4$ with eigenvector $(1,1,1)$.

**Trace and determinant.** $\operatorname{tr}(A)=6$ and $\det A=4$ (direct computation).

**Remaining eigenvalues.** Let $\lambda_2,\lambda_3$ be the other eigenvalues. Then:
$$\lambda_2+\lambda_3=6-4=2, \qquad \lambda_2\lambda_3=\frac{\det A}{\lambda_1}=\frac44=1.$$
The solutions are $\lambda_2=\lambda_3=1$.

**Conclusion.** Eigenvalues $4,1,1$. The eigenspace $E_1$ is the plane $x+y+z=0$, of dimension $2$.

@@@ eigenvalues-characteristic-polynomial.md
---
title: The characteristic polynomial
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: characteristic polynomial, eigenvalues, trace, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the characteristic polynomial of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$$
and verify the trace and determinant formulas.

## Hint

For a triangular matrix, $\chi_A(\lambda)=(\lambda_1-\lambda)(\lambda_2-\lambda)(\lambda_3-\lambda)$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.** $A$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $1,3,4$, so
$$\chi_A(\lambda) = (1-\lambda)(3-\lambda)(4-\lambda).$$
Expanding:
$$(1-\lambda)(3-\lambda)=3-4\lambda+\lambda^2,$$
$$(3-4\lambda+\lambda^2)(4-\lambda)=12-3\lambda-16\lambda+4\lambda^2+4\lambda^2-\lambda^3=12-19\lambda+8\lambda^2-\lambda^3.$$
So $\chi_A(\lambda)=-\lambda^3+8\lambda^2-19\lambda+12$.

**Check.** Trace: coefficient of $\lambda^2$ is $8=\operatorname{tr}(A)$. Constant term: $-(-1)^3\cdot 12 = 12=\det A$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $\chi_A(\lambda)=-(\lambda-1)(\lambda-3)(\lambda-4)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalization-2x2.md
---
title: Diagonalizing a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalization, change of basis, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Diagonalize
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 1 \\ 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix},$$
i.e. find an invertible $P$ and a diagonal $D$ with $A=PDP^{-1}$.

## Hint

Use the eigenvalues $2$ and $5$ and the corresponding eigenvectors found earlier.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues and eigenvectors.** From the earlier exercise: $\lambda_1=2$ with $v_1=(1,-2)$, and $\lambda_2=5$ with $v_2=(1,1)$.

**Matrix $P$.** The columns of $P$ are the eigenvectors:
$$P = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Inverse of $P$.** $\det P=1\cdot1-1\cdot(-2)=3$, so
$$P^{-1}=\frac13\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Diagonal matrix.**
$$D = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $PDP^{-1}=A$ (direct computation).

**Conclusion.** $A=PDP^{-1}$ with the matrices above. Consequently $A^k=PD^kP^{-1}$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalization-3x3.md
---
title: Diagonalizing a 3Ã—3 matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalization, eigenspaces, change of basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Diagonalize
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Use the eigenvalues $4,1,1$ found earlier. Find a basis of each eigenspace and form the matrix $P$.

## Solution

**Eigenspaces.**
- $E_4 = \ker(A-4I)$ has dimension $1$, spanned by $v_1=(1,1,1)$.
- $E_1 = \ker(A-I)$ has dimension $2$, spanned by $v_2=(1,-1,0)$ and $v_3=(1,0,-1)$.

**Diagonalization.**
$$P = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad D = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The columns of $P$ are linearly independent, so $P$ is invertible and $A=PDP^{-1}$.

**Conclusion.** $A$ is diagonalizable with eigenvalues $4,1,1$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalizability-criterion.md
---
title: The diagonalizability criterion
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalizability, multiplicity, eigenspaces
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix. Show that $A$ is diagonalizable if and only if:
1. the characteristic polynomial splits into linear factors over $\mathbb{R}$;
2. for each eigenvalue $\lambda$, its geometric multiplicity $\dim E_\lambda$ equals its algebraic multiplicity (the multiplicity of $\lambda$ as a root of $\chi_A$).

## Hint

If $A$ is diagonalizable, it is similar to a diagonal matrix $D$, and the eigenspaces of $A$ correspond to those of $D$ via the change of basis.

## Solution

**Forward direction.** If $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D$ diagonal, the diagonal entries of $D$ are the eigenvalues of $A$, and each eigenvalue $\lambda$ appears on the diagonal exactly as many times as its algebraic multiplicity. The corresponding eigenspace has the same dimension as the eigenspace of $D$ for $\lambda$, which is the number of times $\lambda$ appears on the diagonal. So geometric multiplicity equals algebraic multiplicity.

**Converse.** Suppose the characteristic polynomial splits and each geometric multiplicity equals algebraic multiplicity. Then the sum of the geometric multiplicities equals the sum of the algebraic multiplicities, which equals $n$. Since the eigenspaces for distinct eigenvalues are in direct sum, the union of bases of the eigenspaces is a basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$, so $A$ is diagonalizable.

**Conclusion.** The two conditions are necessary and sufficient.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalizability-parameter.md
---
title: Diagonalizability depending on a parameter
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: diagonalizability, parameter, eigenspaces
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which real values of $a$ is the matrix
$$A_a = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & a \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$$
diagonalizable?

## Hint

The only eigenvalue is $1$ (double). Compute $\dim\ker(A_a-I)$.

## Solution

**Eigenvalue.** The matrix is upper triangular with both diagonal entries $1$, so $\lambda=1$ is the only eigenvalue, with algebraic multiplicity $2$.

**Eigenspace.**
$$A_a-I = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & a \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
- If $a\neq0$: the kernel is $\{(x,0)\}$, dimension $1$.
- If $a=0$: $A_0=I$, and the kernel is $\mathbb{R}^2$, dimension $2$.

**Conclusion.**
- If $a\neq0$: geometric multiplicity $1<2$, so $A_a$ is **not diagonalizable**.
- If $a=0$: $A_0=I$ is already diagonal, hence diagonalizable.

@@@ eigenvalues-symmetric-orthogonal.md
---
title: Orthogonal diagonalization of a symmetric matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: symmetric matrix, orthogonal diagonalization, spectral theorem
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Orthogonally diagonalize
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Find the eigenvalues, then orthonormalize the eigenvectors.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(A-\lambda I)=(3-\lambda)^2-1=\lambda^2-6\lambda+8=(\lambda-2)(\lambda-4)$. So $\lambda_1=2$ and $\lambda_2=4$.

**Eigenvectors.**
- For $\lambda=2$: $v_1=(1,-1)$.
- For $\lambda=4$: $v_2=(1,1)$.

These are already orthogonal.

**Normalization.** $u_1=(1,-1)/\sqrt2$ and $u_2=(1,1)/\sqrt2$.

**Orthogonal matrix $Q$.**
$$Q = \frac{1}{\sqrt2}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Then $Q^TQ=I$ and
$$Q^TAQ = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** $A=Q\operatorname{diag}(2,4)Q^T$ with $Q$ orthogonal.

@@@ eigenvalues-eigenspace-basis.md

title: A basis of an eigenspace
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenspace, basis, dimension
date: 2026-11-03

## Statement

Let
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Find a basis of the eigenspace $E_2$.

## Hint

$E_2=\ker(A-2I)$. Solve the system.

## Solution

**Kernel.**
$$A-2I = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
The system is $y=0$ and $z=0$, so the kernel is $\{(x,0,0)\}$.

**Conclusion.** $E_2=\operatorname{span}((1,0,0))$, dimension $1$.

**Remark.** The algebraic multiplicity of $\lambda=2$ is $2$ (it appears twice on the diagonal), but the geometric multiplicity is $1$, so $A$ is not diagonalizable.

@@@ eigenvalues-trace-determinant.md
---
title: Trace and determinant of a 3Ã—3 matrix via eigenvalues
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: trace, determinant, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be a $3\times3$ matrix with eigenvalues $2,-1,4$. Compute $\operatorname{tr}(A)$, $\det(A)$, and the characteristic polynomial of $A$.

## Hint

Trace = sum of eigenvalues, determinant = product of eigenvalues.

## Solution

**Trace.** $\operatorname{tr}(A)=2+(-1)+4=5$.

**Determinant.** $\det(A)=2\cdot(-1)\cdot4=-8$.

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\chi_A(\lambda)=(\lambda-2)(\lambda+1)(\lambda-4).$$
Expanding:
$$(\lambda-2)(\lambda+1)=\lambda^2-\lambda-2,$$
$$(\lambda^2-\lambda-2)(\lambda-4)=\lambda^3-5\lambda^2+2\lambda+8.$$
So $\chi_A(\lambda)=\lambda^3-5\lambda^2+2\lambda+8$.

**Check.** Coefficient of $\lambda^2$ is $-\operatorname{tr}(A)=-5$, constant term is $\det(A)=8$. Correct.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalizable-projection.md
---
title: A projection is diagonalizable
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: projection, diagonalization, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $p:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ be a projection, i.e. $p^2=p$. Show that $p$ is diagonalizable and determine its eigenvalues.

## Hint

If $p^2=p$, then $p(p-\mathrm{id})=0$, so the minimal polynomial of $p$ divides $X(X-1)$.

## Solution

**Minimal polynomial.** From $p^2=p$: $p^2-p=0$, so $p(p-\mathrm{id})=0$. The minimal polynomial $\mu_p$ divides $X(X-1)$.

**Eigenvalues.** The possible eigenvalues are roots of $\mu_p$: $0$ and $1$.

**Diagonalizability.** The minimal polynomial has simple roots (no repeated factor), so $p$ is diagonalizable. Its eigenvalues are $0$ and $1$.

**Eigenspaces.** $E_1=\operatorname{Im} p$ (fixed points), $E_0=\ker p$. Moreover $\mathbb{R}^n=E_0\oplus E_1$.

**Conclusion.** Any projection is diagonalizable with eigenvalues $0$ and $1$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalizable-involution.md
---
title: An involution is diagonalizable
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: involution, diagonalization, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ be a linear map with $f^2=\mathrm{id}$. Show that $f$ is diagonalizable and determine its eigenvalues.

## Hint

$f^2-\mathrm{id}=0$, so $(f-\mathrm{id})(f+\mathrm{id})=0$. The minimal polynomial divides $X^2-1=(X-1)(X+1)$.

## Solution

**Minimal polynomial.** $f^2=\mathrm{id}$ implies $f^2-\mathrm{id}=0$, so the minimal polynomial $\mu_f$ divides $X^2-1$.

**Eigenvalues.** The roots of $\mu_f$ are among $1$ and $-1$. If $f=\mathrm{id}$, the only eigenvalue is $1$; if $f=-\mathrm{id}$, the only eigenvalue is $-1$; otherwise both are eigenvalues.

**Diagonalizability.** $X^2-1$ has simple roots (in characteristic $\neq2$), so $\mu_f$ also has simple roots and $f$ is diagonalizable.

**Eigenspaces.** $E_1=\ker(f-\mathrm{id})$, $E_{-1}=\ker(f+\mathrm{id})$, and $\mathbb{R}^n=E_1\oplus E_{-1}$.

**Conclusion.** Any involution is diagonalizable with eigenvalues in $\{1,-1\}$.

@@@ eigenvalues-multiplicity.md
---
title: Geometric versus algebraic multiplicity
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: multiplicities, eigenspace, diagonalization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix. Show that for every eigenvalue $\lambda$,
$$1\le\dim E_\lambda\le m_\lambda,$$
where $m_\lambda$ is the algebraic multiplicity of $\lambda$ (as root of $\chi_A$).

## Hint

The first inequality is because an eigenvalue has a nonzero eigenvector. For the second, use the rank-nullity theorem and properties of the characteristic polynomial.

## Solution

**Lower bound.** By definition, an eigenvalue has at least one nonzero eigenvector, so $\dim E_\lambda\ge1$.

**Upper bound.** Let $k=\dim E_\lambda$ and choose a basis $(v_1,\ldots,v_k)$ of $E_\lambda$. Extend this basis to a basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$. In this basis, the matrix of $A$ is block upper triangular:
$$\begin{pmatrix} \lambda I_k & * \\ 0 & * \end{pmatrix}.$$
Therefore $\chi_A$ has the factor $(\lambda-X)^k$, i.e. the algebraic multiplicity of $\lambda$ is at least $k$. Hence $k\le m_\lambda$.

**Conclusion.** $1\le\dim E_\lambda\le m_\lambda$. Equality for all $\lambda$ iff $A$ is diagonalizable.

@@@ eigenvalues-cayley-hamilton.md
---
title: The Cayleyâ€“Hamilton theorem
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Cayley-Hamilton, characteristic polynomial, matrix identity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State and verify the Cayleyâ€“Hamilton theorem on the example
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

The theorem states that $\chi_A(A)=0$. Compute $\chi_A$, then evaluate at $A$.

## Solution

**Theorem.** Every square matrix satisfies its own characteristic polynomial: $\chi_A(A)=0$.

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\chi_A(\lambda)=\det(A-\lambda I)=(2-\lambda)^2-1=\lambda^2-4\lambda+3.$$

**Verification.**
$$A^2 = \begin{pmatrix} 5 & 4 \\ 4 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Then
$$\chi_A(A)=A^2-4A+3I = \begin{pmatrix} 5 & 4 \\ 4 & 5 \end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix} 8 & 4 \\ 4 & 8 \end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** $\chi_A(A)=0$, confirming Cayleyâ€“Hamilton. This gives $A^2=4A-3I$, allowing recursive computation of $A^k$.

@@@ eigenvalues-companion-fibonacci.md
---
title: Powers of a companion matrix and Fibonacci numbers
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: companion matrix, Fibonacci, diagonalization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Diagonalize $A$ and deduce a closed-form formula for the Fibonacci numbers $F_n$ defined by $F_0=0$, $F_1=1$, $F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n$.

## Hint

Eigenvalues solve $\lambda^2=\lambda+1$, i.e. $\lambda=\varphi$ or $\lambda=\psi$ with $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$, $\psi=(1-\sqrt5)/2$.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(A-\lambda I)=\lambda^2-\lambda-1$, with roots $\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}$ and $\psi=\frac{1-\sqrt5}{2}$.

**Eigenvectors.**
- For $\varphi$: $v_1=(1,\varphi)$.
- For $\psi$: $v_2=(1,\psi)$.

**Diagonalization.** $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\\varphi&\psi\end{pmatrix}$ and $D=\operatorname{diag}(\varphi,\psi)$.

**Fibonacci relation.** $\begin{pmatrix}F_{n+1}\\F_n\end{pmatrix}=A^n\begin{pmatrix}F_1\\F_0\end{pmatrix}=A^n\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}$.

**Closed formula.** From $A^n=P D^n P^{-1}$, we get
$$F_n=\frac{\varphi^n-\psi^n}{\sqrt5}.$$

**Check.** $F_0=0$, $F_1=1$, $F_2=1$, $F_3=2$, consistent with the Binet formula.

**Conclusion.** $F_n=\frac{1}{\sqrt5}\left[\left(\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}\right)^n-\left(\frac{1-\sqrt5}{2}\right)^n\right]$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalizable-trigonalizable.md
---
title: Trigonalization and eigenvalues
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: trigonalization, characteristic polynomial, real eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ real matrix. Show that $A$ is trigonalizable over $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if its characteristic polynomial has all roots in $\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Use induction on $n$: find a real eigenvalue, which gives a stable line, then apply the induction hypothesis to the quotient.

## Solution

**Forward direction.** If $A$ is similar to an upper triangular matrix $T$, then $\chi_A=\chi_T$, which is a product of linear factors, so all roots are real.

**Converse.** Suppose $\chi_A$ has all roots in $\mathbb{R}$. Then $A$ has at least one real eigenvalue $\lambda_1$, hence a stable line $L_1=\operatorname{span}(v_1)$ with $Av_1=\lambda_1 v_1$. Complete $(v_1)$ to a basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$. In this basis, the matrix of $A$ is block upper triangular:
$$M = \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 & * \\ 0 & B \end{pmatrix}.$$
The characteristic polynomial of $B$ divides $\chi_A$, so it also has all roots in $\mathbb{R}$. By induction, $B$ is trigonalizable, and consequently so is $M$.

**Conclusion.** Trigonalizability over $\mathbb{R}$ is equivalent to all eigenvalues being real.

@@@ eigenvalues-eigenvector-subspace.md
---
title: Eigenvectors for a given eigenvalue form a subspace
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenspace, subspace, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix and $\lambda$ a real number. Show that
$$E_\lambda = \{v\in\mathbb{R}^n : Av=\lambda v\}$$
is a subspace of $\mathbb{R}^n$.

## Hint

$E_\lambda=\ker(A-\lambda I)$.

## Solution

**Identification.** $Av=\lambda v\iff(A-\lambda I)v=0\iff v\in\ker(A-\lambda I)$.

**Subspace.** The kernel of a linear map is a subspace. Indeed, $0\in\ker(A-\lambda I)$; if $v,w\in E_\lambda$, then $(A-\lambda I)(v+w)=0+0=0$; if $\alpha\in\mathbb{R}$ and $v\in E_\lambda$, then $(A-\lambda I)(\alpha v)=\alpha\cdot0=0$.

**Conclusion.** $E_\lambda$ is a subspace, called the **eigenspace** of $A$ for $\lambda$.

**Note.** If $\lambda$ is not an eigenvalue, $E_\lambda=\{0\}$.

@@@ eigenvalues-sum-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Sum of eigenvalues equals trace
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenvalues, trace, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix with eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n$ (counted with multiplicity). Show that
$$\sum_i\lambda_i = \operatorname{tr}(A), \qquad \prod_i\lambda_i = \det(A).$$

## Hint

Expand $\chi_A(\lambda)=\prod(\lambda-\lambda_i)$ and compare coefficients with $\chi_A(\lambda)=\lambda^n-\operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda^{n-1}+\cdots+(-1)^n\det A$.

## Solution

**Expansion of $\prod(\lambda-\lambda_i)$.** The coefficient of $\lambda^{n-1}$ is $-\sum_i\lambda_i$. The constant term is $(-1)^n\prod_i\lambda_i$.

**Identification.** Comparing with
$$\chi_A(\lambda)=\lambda^n-\operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda^{n-1}+\cdots+(-1)^n\det A,$$
we get
$$\sum_i\lambda_i=\operatorname{tr}(A), \qquad \prod_i\lambda_i=\det A.$$

**Conclusion.** Trace and determinant are the elementary symmetric functions of degree $1$ and $n$ of the eigenvalues.

## Statement

Let $R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$ be a rotation. Compute its eigenvalues (real or complex) as a function of $\theta$.

## Hint

$\det(R_\theta-\lambda I)=\lambda^2-2\cos\theta\cdot\lambda+1$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\det(R_\theta-\lambda I)=\lambda^2-2\cos\theta\cdot\lambda+1.$$
Discriminant: $\Delta=4\cos^2\theta-4=-4\sin^2\theta$.

**Case $\sin\theta=0$ (Î¸=0 or Ï€).**
- $\theta=0$: $R=I$, $\lambda=1$ (double).
- $\theta=\pi$: $R=-I$, $\lambda=-1$ (double).

**Case $\sin\theta\neq0$.** Complex eigenvalues:
$$\lambda_{\pm}=e^{\pm i\theta}=\cos\theta\pm i\sin\theta.$$

**Conclusion.** A rotation of angle $\theta\notin\{0,\pi\}$ has no real eigenvalues but complex eigenvalues $e^{\pm i\theta}$. This reflects the fact that a non-trivial rotation has no invariant line.

## Statement

Let
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Find the eigenvalues of $A$ and $\dim E_3$. Is $A$ diagonalizable?

## Hint

The matrix is upper triangular with diagonal $3,3,3$. Compute $\ker(A-3I)$.

## Solution

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalization-criterion-minimal.md
---
title: Diagonalizability and the minimal polynomial
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: minimal polynomial, diagonalization, criterion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix. Show that $A$ is diagonalizable if and only if its minimal polynomial has only simple roots.

## Hint

If $A$ is diagonalizable, the minimal polynomial is $\prod(X-\lambda_i)$ over distinct eigenvalues. Conversely, if the minimal polynomial has simple roots, use the kernel decomposition.

## Solution

**Forward direction.** If $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$, then $A$ is killed by $\prod_i(X-\lambda_i)$ where the product is over distinct eigenvalues. The minimal polynomial is the smallest such polynomial, so it is a product of distinct linear factors.

**Converse.** Let $\mu_A(X)=\prod_{i=1}^r(X-\lambda_i)$ with distinct $\lambda_i$. For each $i$, let $E_i=\ker(A-\lambda_i I)$. We claim that the sum $E_1+\cdots+E_r$ is $\mathbb{R}^n$.

Indeed, the polynomials $P_i(X)=\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{X-\lambda_j}{\lambda_i-\lambda_j}$ form a Lagrange interpolation basis: $\sum_i P_i=1$ and $P_i$ is zero at all $\lambda_j$ for $j\neq i$, and $P_i(\lambda_i)=1$.

For any $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$: $v=\sum_i P_i(A)v$ (since $\sum_i P_i=1$). But $(A-\lambda_i I)P_i(A)v = \mu_A(A)\cdot(\text{something})=0$, so $P_i(A)v\in E_i$. Hence $v$ decomposes as a sum of eigenvectors, and $\mathbb{R}^n$ is spanned by the eigenspaces. Thus $A$ is diagonalizable.

**Conclusion.** $A$ is diagonalizable iff its minimal polynomial has only simple roots.

@@@ eigenvalues-circulant.md
---
title: Eigenvalues of a circulant matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: circulant matrix, eigenvalues, roots of unity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$C = \begin{pmatrix} a & b & c \\ c & a & b \\ b & c & a \end{pmatrix}.$$
Show that the eigenvalues of $C$ are
$$\lambda_k = a + b\omega^k + c\omega^{2k}, \qquad k=0,1,2,$$
where $\omega=e^{2i\pi/3}$, with eigenvectors $(1,\omega^k,\omega^{2k})$.

## Hint

Compute $C v$ for $v=(1,\omega,\omega^2)$ and use $\omega^3=1$, $1+\omega+\omega^2=0$.

## Solution

**Computation.** Let $v_k=(1,\omega^k,\omega^{2k})^T$. Then
$$(Cv_k)_1 = a\cdot1+b\cdot\omega^k+c\cdot\omega^{2k} = \lambda_k.$$
$$(Cv_k)_2 = c\cdot1+a\cdot\omega^k+b\cdot\omega^{2k} = \omega^k(a+b\omega^k+c\omega^{2k}) = \omega^k\lambda_k.$$
$$(Cv_k)_3 = b\cdot1+c\cdot\omega^k+a\cdot\omega^{2k} = \omega^{2k}(a+b\omega^k+c\omega^{2k}) = \omega^{2k}\lambda_k.$$
So $Cv_k=\lambda_k v_k$, and $\lambda_k=a+b\omega^k+c\omega^{2k}$.

**Diagonalizability.** For $k=0,1,2$, the vectors $v_k$ are linearly independent (Vandermonde-type argument with distinct $\omega^k$). So $C$ is diagonalizable over $\mathbb{C}$, with eigenvalues $\lambda_0,\lambda_1,\lambda_2$.

**Conclusion.** The eigenvalues of the circulant matrix $C$ are given by the discrete Fourier transform of $(a,b,c)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonal-conjugation.md
---
title: Similar matrices have the same eigenvalues
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: similarity, eigenvalues, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ and $B=P^{-1}AP$ be similar matrices. Show that $A$ and $B$ have the same characteristic polynomial, hence the same eigenvalues with the same algebraic multiplicities.

## Hint

Compute $\det(B-\lambda I)$ by factoring $P^{-1}$ and $P$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\det(B-\lambda I)=\det(P^{-1}AP-\lambda I)=\det(P^{-1}(A-\lambda I)P)=\det(P^{-1})\det(A-\lambda I)\det(P).$$
Since $\det(P^{-1})\det(P)=1$, we get $\det(B-\lambda I)=\det(A-\lambda I)$.

**Consequence.** $\chi_A=\chi_B$, so $A$ and $B$ have the same eigenvalues with the same multiplicities.

**Eigenvectors.** If $Av=\lambda v$, then $B(P^{-1}v)=P^{-1}Av=\lambda P^{-1}v$. So $P^{-1}$ maps eigenspaces of $A$ to eigenspaces of $B$. In particular, $\dim E_\lambda(A)=\dim E_\lambda(B)$.

**Conclusion.** Similar matrices have identical spectral data.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonal-powers.md
---
title: Powers via diagonalization
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: diagonalization, powers, matrix
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$. Show that for all $k\ge1$,
$$A^k=PD^kP^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1^k,\ldots,\lambda_n^k)$$
in the appropriate basis.

## Hint

Prove by induction using $A=PDP^{-1}$.

## Solution

**Induction.** For $k=1$, $A^1=PDP^{-1}$. Suppose $A^k=PD^kP^{-1}$. Then
$$A^{k+1}=A^k\cdot A=PD^kP^{-1}\cdot PDP^{-1}=PD^kDP^{-1}=PD^{k+1}P^{-1}.$$

**Diagonal power.** $D^k=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1^k,\ldots,\lambda_n^k)$.

**Conclusion.** $A^k=PD^kP^{-1}$. This formula is used to compute powers efficiently once $A$ is diagonalized.

**Application.** If $D=\operatorname{diag}(2,3)$ and $P=I$, then $A^k=\operatorname{diag}(2^k,3^k)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-spectral-decomposition.md
---
title: Spectral decomposition of a symmetric matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: spectral decomposition, symmetric matrix, projection
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be a real symmetric matrix with distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r$. Let $P_i$ be the orthogonal projection onto the eigenspace $E_{\lambda_i}$. Show that
$$A=\sum_{i=1}^r\lambda_i P_i, \qquad \sum_{i=1}^r P_i = I.$$

## Hint

In an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors, $A$ is diagonal with eigenvalues on the diagonal; interpret the diagonal entries via the projections.

## Solution

**Orthonormal eigenbasis.** Since $A$ is symmetric, there is an orthonormal basis $(u_1,\ldots,u_n)$ of eigenvectors, with $Au_j=\mu_j u_j$ (where $\mu_j$ ranges over the $\lambda_i$).

**Projections.** $P_i$ is the orthogonal projection onto $E_{\lambda_i}$: for any $v$, $P_i v$ is the sum of the components of $v$ along eigenvectors with eigenvalue $\lambda_i$.

**Spectral decomposition.** For any $v$:
$$Av=\sum_j \mu_j\langle v,u_j\rangle u_j=\sum_i \lambda_i\sum_{j:\mu_j=\lambda_i}\langle v,u_j\rangle u_j=\sum_i\lambda_i P_i v.$$
So $A=\sum_i\lambda_i P_i$.

**Completeness.** $\sum_i P_i$ projects onto the sum of the eigenspaces, which is $\mathbb{R}^n$, so $\sum_i P_i=I$.

**Conclusion.** Symmetric matrices admit a spectral decomposition as a sum of orthogonal projections scaled by eigenvalues.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonal-functions.md
---
title: Functions of a diagonalizable matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: functions of matrices, diagonalization, polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$. Define $f(A)=P f(D) P^{-1}$ where $f(D)=\operatorname{diag}(f(\lambda_1),\ldots,f(\lambda_n))$ for a function $f$ defined on the eigenvalues. Justify this definition for polynomials $f$.

## Hint

For a polynomial $f(X)=\sum_k a_k X^k$, $f(A)=\sum_k a_k A^k$.

## Solution

**Polynomial case.** For $f(X)=\sum_k a_k X^k$:
$$f(A)=\sum_k a_k A^k=\sum_k a_k PD^kP^{-1}=P\left(\sum_k a_k D^k\right)P^{-1}=Pf(D)P^{-1}.$$
So the definition is consistent with the polynomial case.

**Extension.** For other functions (e.g. $\exp$, $\sin$, $\log$), the same formula defines $f(A)$ provided $f$ is defined on the spectrum of $A$. This requires $A$ to be diagonalizable.

**Example.** $e^{tA}=P\operatorname{diag}(e^{t\lambda_1},\ldots,e^{t\lambda_n})P^{-1}$.

**Conclusion.** Functions of a diagonalizable matrix are defined via the diagonal form. For non-diagonalizable matrices, one uses Taylor or Jordan form instead.

@@@ determinant-2x2-basic.md
---
title: Determinant of a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: determinant, 2x2 matrices, invertibility
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of each matrix and decide whether it is invertible:

(a) $A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 5 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$;
(b) $B = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$;
(c) $C = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta \\ \sin\theta & \cos\theta \end{pmatrix}$.

## Hint

For a $2\times2$ matrix $\begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{pmatrix}$, the determinant is $ad-bc$. The matrix is invertible iff the determinant is nonzero.

## Solution

**(a)** $\det A = 3\cdot2 - 1\cdot5 = 6-5=1$. Invertible.

**(b)** $\det B = 1\cdot4 - 2\cdot2 = 0$. Not invertible.

**(c)** $\det C = \cos\theta\cdot\cos\theta - (-\sin\theta)(\sin\theta) = \cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta = 1$. Invertible for all $\theta$ (it is a rotation matrix).

@@@ determinant-3x3-sarrus.md
---
title: Determinant of a 3Ã—3 matrix by Sarrus' rule
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: determinant, Sarrus rule, 3x3 matrices
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 4 & 5 & 6 \\ 7 & 8 & 10 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Use Sarrus' rule: add the three products along downward diagonals and subtract the three products along upward diagonals.

## Solution

**Sarrus' rule.**
$$\det A = 1\cdot5\cdot10 + 2\cdot6\cdot7 + 3\cdot4\cdot8 - 3\cdot5\cdot7 - 1\cdot6\cdot8 - 2\cdot4\cdot10.$$
Compute each term:
$$= 50 + 84 + 96 - 105 - 48 - 80 = 230 - 233 = -3.$$

**Conclusion.** $\det A = -3$, so $A$ is invertible.

@@@ determinant-expansion-row.md
---
title: Expansion along a row
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: cofactor expansion, minors, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 5 & 0 & 0 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$$
by expanding along the most convenient row or column.

## Hint

Expand along the second row, which has only one nonzero entry.

## Solution

**Expansion along the second row.** The only nonzero entry is $a_{22}=3$. Its cofactor is $(-1)^{2+2}=+1$ times the determinant of the $3\times3$ minor obtained by deleting row $2$ and column $2$:
$$M_{22} = \begin{vmatrix} 2 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 5 & 0 & 6 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Expand $M_{22}$ along its second row.** The only nonzero entry is $4$ at position $(2,2)$, cofactor $(+1)\begin{vmatrix}2&1\\5&6\end{vmatrix} = 12-5=7$.

So $M_{22} = 4\cdot7 = 28$.

**Conclusion.** $\det A = 3\cdot28 = 84$.

**Check by permutation formula.** The matrix is a block "diagonal with a corner" structure; the result $2\cdot3\cdot4\cdot6 + (\text{sign})\cdot1\cdot3\cdot4\cdot5 = 144 - 60 = 84$ confirms it.

@@@ determinant-expansion-column.md
---
title: Expansion along a column
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: cofactor expansion, minors, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 0 & 4 & 5 \\ 1 & 0 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$$
by expanding along the first column.

## Hint

Use the cofactor expansion along column $1$: $\det A = \sum_i (-1)^{i+1}a_{i1}\det M_{i1}$.

## Solution

**Expansion along column 1.**
$$\det A = 1\cdot\begin{vmatrix}4&5\\0&6\end{vmatrix} - 0\cdot\begin{vmatrix}2&3\\0&6\end{vmatrix} + 1\cdot\begin{vmatrix}2&3\\4&5\end{vmatrix}.$$

**Minor computations.**
$$\begin{vmatrix}4&5\\0&6\end{vmatrix} = 24, \qquad \begin{vmatrix}2&3\\4&5\end{vmatrix} = 10-12=-2.$$

**Conclusion.** $\det A = 1\cdot24 + 1\cdot(-2) = 22$.

**Check by Sarrus.** $1\cdot4\cdot6 + 2\cdot5\cdot1 + 3\cdot0\cdot0 - 3\cdot4\cdot1 - 2\cdot0\cdot6 - 1\cdot5\cdot0 = 24+10+0-12-0-0 = 22$. Consistent.

@@@ determinant-row-operation-effect.md
---
title: Effect of row operations on the determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: row operations, determinant properties, multilinearity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the effect of each elementary row operation on the determinant:

1. $L_i \leftrightarrow L_j$ (swap two rows);
2. $L_i \leftarrow \lambda L_i$ with $\lambda\neq 0$ (multiply a row by $\lambda$);
3. $L_i \leftarrow L_i + \lambda L_j$ with $j\neq i$ (add a multiple of another row).

## Hint

Use multilinearity and the alternating property of the determinant.

## Solution

**1. Swapping.** The determinant changes sign: $\det = -\det_{\text{original}}$. This follows from the alternating property (the determinant is zero when two rows are equal, and swapping is an odd permutation).

**2. Scaling.** Multiplying a row by $\lambda$ multiplies the determinant by $\lambda$: $\det = \lambda\cdot\det_{\text{original}}$. This follows from linearity in each row.

**3. Adding a multiple.** Adding a multiple of one row to another leaves the determinant unchanged.
